Strict universes for Grothendieck topoi
Daniel Gratzer, Michael Shulman, Jonathan Sterling

Abstract
Hofmann and Streicher famously showed how to lift Grothendieck universes into presheaf topoi, and Streicher has extended their result to the case of sheaf topoi by sheafification. In parallel, van den Berg and Moerdijk have shown in the context of algebraic set theory that similar constructions continue to apply even in weaker metatheories. Unfortunately, sheafification seems not to preserve an important realignment property enjoyed by presheaf universes that plays a critical role in models of univalent type theory as well as synthetic Tait computability. When multiple universes are present, realignment also implies a coherent interpretation of connectives across all universes that justifies the cumulativity laws present in popular formulations of Martin-Löf type theory. We observe that a slight adjustment to an argument of Shulman lifts a well-behaved cumulative universe hierarchy in the category of sets to a cumulative universe hierarchy satisfying the realignment property at every level in any Grothendieck topos. Hence one has direct interpretations of Martin-Löf type theory with cumulative universes into all Grothendieck topoi. A further implication is to extend the reach of recent synthetic methods in the semantics of cubical type theory and the syntactic metatheory of type theory and programming languages to all Grothendieck topoi.

1
Introduction

Grothendieck introduced the language of
universes
to control the
size issues that plague a naïve categorical development of algebraic
geometry
[
AGV72
]
. In a somewhat different line of research, Martin-Löf
introduced universes into dependent type theory as a
reflection
principle

[
Mar71
,
Mar75
,
Mar79
,
Mar84
]
.
In either case a universe parameterizes a class of maps that are closed under
enough operations to do mathematics, including dependent product/sum,
quotients,
etc
.

Grothendieck’s use of universes was located in the ambient set theory; each
universe
\mathscr{U}
determines a category of
\mathscr{U}
-small sets and
functions that serves as a base for both enrichment and internalization,
generalizing the notions of locally small and small category respectively. The
past three decades have however seen an increased interest in the adaptation of
universes to categories other than
Set
:

1.

Universes play a central role in the
algebraic
set theory
of
[
JM95
]
, which explores the relationship between sets and classes from a categorical viewpoint.

2.

Voevodsky’s elucidation of the univalence
principle
[
Voe06
]
, foreshadowed
by
[
HS98
]
, has reinvigorated the study of universes in
topoi. Closely related to Voevodsky’s univalent universes are the

object classifiers
of
\infty
-topos theory in the
Joyal–Lurie–Rezk tradition
[
Lur09
,
Rez10
]
.

3.

It is of practical interest to employ Martin-Löf type theory (MLTT)
as an internal language for a variety of categories. In
addition to the standard applications of internal methods to mathematics,
the existence of topos models of MLTT is a critical ingredient for a
number of recent results in type theory and programming languages,
including the generalized abstraction theorem of

[
SH21
]
and the proofs of normalization for cubical
type theory and multi-modal dependent type
theory
[
SA21
,
Gra22
]
.

Unfortunately some doubt has proliferated in the type theoretic literature (
e.g
.

[
Xu15
,
XE16
,
CMR17
]
) as to when sufficiently well-adapted
universes exist in a topos. It is a well-known result of

[
HS97
]
that Grothendieck universes can be lifted
pointwise into presheaf topoi, and it is slightly less well-known that
sheafification preserves all the properties of this universe that do not
involve strict equality of codes
[
Str05
,
Ber11
]
. Such a sheafified
universe is already sufficient for nearly all mathematical purposes, but falls
short in applications to the semantics and metatheory of dependent type theory,
where certain strict laws not preserved by sheafification remain important.

In this paper we expose an alternative universe construction that applies
in an arbitrary Grothendieck topos, using nothing but the cocompleteness and
exactness properties of Grothendieck topoi. Ours is a variant of a
construction of a universe of presheaves due to
[
Shu15
]
;
we demonstrate that the resulting universe satisfies an important
realignment
property, which suffices in particular to obtain models of
Martin-Löf type theory with a cumulative hierarchy of universes in any
Grothendieck topos. The realignment condition is also an important ingredient
in the construction of
univalent
universes for models of homotopy type theory
[
Uni13
]
.

1.1
Elementary axioms for universes in a topos

Inspired by the definitions of classes of open and small maps from algebraic
set theory,
[
Str05
]
has given a definition of a universe in an elementary
topos
\mathscr{E}
which we review in
Definition

2
below.

Definition 2
.

A class of arrows
\mathcal{S}\subseteq\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathscr{E}}
is called a
universe

by
[
Str05
]
when it satisfies the following axioms:

(U1)

\mathcal{S}
is pullback-stable,
i.e
.
if
f\in\mathcal{S}
and

g
f
is a cartesian square, then
g\in\mathcal{S}
.

(U2)

\mathcal{S}
contains all monomorphisms in
\mathscr{E}
.

(U3)

\mathcal{S}
is closed under composition.

(U4)

(U5)

There exists a
generic
morphism,
i.e

dependent type

x:U\vdash E\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\displaystyle x$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\textstyle x$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\scriptstyle x$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle x$}\mathclose{}{]}}

In the type-theoretic literature, it is the base of this family
U
which is
called the universe and the generic family is the dependent type
\mathsf{El}

rendering an element of this universe as a genuine type. We occasionally adopt
this terminology and blur the distinction between a universe and its generic map
by referring to
E
U
simply as a universe. Some caution is required:
while a generic map uniquely determines a universe, the converse is not
necessarily true and a universe can have multiple distinct generic maps.

In the context of Martin-Löf type theory, it is common to study classes of
maps that may not satisfy all the axioms above; for instance, type theory is
often used in settings that do not have a single well-behaved notion of
proposition, so
(U2)
loses some significance. We therefore define a
notion of
pre-universe
below.

Definition 3
.

A
pre-universe
is a class of arrows satisfying axioms
(U1,U3–5)
.

[

]
discusses some additional useful but optional axioms for universes.

The first result in particular has been widely used in the semantics of type theory, because the
generic morphism satisfies a number of strict equations specific to its construction. These
additional equations are crucial for modeling

strict cumulative universes. Other more novel
applications of this strictness have emerged in models of Voevodsky’s univalence axiom and homotopy
type theory. Only more recently has an axiomatic basis for these stricter Hofmann–Streicher
universes been isolated:

Lemma 7

Suppose

1.2
From realignment to cumulative hierarchies

The true utility of
(U8)
is the ability to choose a representation for a morphism

f\in\mathcal{S}
subject to a strict equation. For instance,
(U8)
is sufficient to ‘strictify’
a hierarchy of universes so that the choices of codes for connectives commute with the coercion maps from
one universe to another
[
Shu15
]
. In particular, let

be two universes equipped with a choice of cartesian monomorphism

P_{\pi_{\mathcal{S}}}\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle\pi_{\mathcal{S}}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle\pi_{\mathcal{S}}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\pi_{\mathcal{S}}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\pi_{\mathcal{S}}$}\mathclose{}{)}}

\pi_{\mathcal{S}}
\pi_{\mathcal{T}}
\Pi_{\mathcal{S}}
i
\Pi_{\mathcal{T}}

(1)

We can replace
\Pi_{\mathcal{S}},\Pi_{\mathcal{T}}
with new codes

\Pi_{\mathcal{S}}^{\prime},\Pi_{\mathcal{T}}^{\prime}
for which the analogue to

Eq.

1
commutes. We set
\Pi_{\mathcal{S}}^{\prime}\coloneqq\Pi_{\mathcal{S}}
and define
\Pi_{\mathcal{T}}^{\prime}
by realigning

i\circ\Pi^{\prime}_{\mathcal{S}}
along
P_{i}\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle i$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle i$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle i$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle i$}\mathclose{}{)}}
:

P_{\pi_{\mathcal{S}}}\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle\pi_{\mathcal{S}}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle\pi_{\mathcal{S}}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\pi_{\mathcal{S}}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\pi_{\mathcal{S}}$}\mathclose{}{)}}
P_{\pi_{\mathcal{T}}}\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle\pi_{\mathcal{T}}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle\pi_{\mathcal{T}}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\pi_{\mathcal{T}}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\pi_{\mathcal{T}}$}\mathclose{}{)}}
P_{i}\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle i$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle i$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle i$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle i$}\mathclose{}{)}}
\pi_{\mathcal{S}}
\pi_{\mathcal{T}}
\Pi_{\mathcal{S}}^{\prime}
i
\Pi_{\mathcal{T}}^{\prime}

(2)

If we further assume that
\mathscr{E}
is sufficiently cocomplete,
e.g
.
, if it is a
Grothendieck topos, the technique above easily extends to infinite and even
transfinite hierarchies of universes. In the latter case, one realigns along the

join
of all the subobjects

P_{\pi_{\mathcal{S}^{\prime}_{i}}}\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle\pi_{\mathcal{S}^{\prime}_{i}}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle\pi_{\mathcal{S}^{\prime}_{i}}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\pi_{\mathcal{S}^{\prime}_{i}}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\pi_{\mathcal{S}^{\prime}_{i}}$}\mathclose{}{)}}

pertaining to the formation data for dependent product type codes at lower
universes. Then a coherent hierarchy of such codes is built “from the ground
up” by induction.

1.3
Structure of the paper

We survey the landscape of universe constructions available in Grothendieck toposes and show that
they inherit a plentiful supply of well-behaved universes from
Set
.

Section

2
. We revisit the presheaf-theoretic universe
construction of
[
HS97
]
, lifting a Grothendieck universe
in
Set
to a universe of pointwise small families of presheaves satisfying

(U1–8)
. Presenting a sheaf topos as a subcategory of a presheaf
topos, we recall from
[
Str05
]
that the Hofmann–Streicher
construction also produces universes of sheaves satisfying
(U1–6)
,
as the sheafification of the generic small family of presheaves is generic for
small families of sheaves.

Section

3
. We review a number of categorical preliminaries
to our main result involving descent and
\kappa
-compactness.

Section

4
. Adapting a construction of

[
Shu15
]
, we prove our main result
(
Corollary

64
): the universe of relatively

\kappa
-compact sheaves for a strongly inaccessible cardinal
\kappa

satisfies all the universe axioms including
(U8)
. We deduce that
cumulative hierarchies of strict universes lift from
Set
to any Grothendieck
topos.

Section

5
. We discuss and compare two
equivalent formulations of the realignment property employing the internal
language of a topos.

Section

6
. The results of
Section

4
have important
consequences for the syntax and semantics of type theory; we review a few of
these applications in
Section

6
. For instance, we have already shown
that
(U8)
is sufficient to construct strictly cumulative hierarchies
of universes, and with the existence of these hierarchies in arbitrary
Grothendieck topoi the independence of several logical principles of
Martin-Löf type theory immediately follows; contrary to some claims, sheaf
semantics is sufficient and there is no need to move from sheaves to stacks. We
outline applications to independence results in
Section

6.1
.

We also illustrate the general utility of
(U8)
through two specific
examples: the semantics of univalence in homotopy type theory (
Section

6.2
)
and the construction of glued models of type theory
(
Section

6.3
) for proving syntactic metatheorems such as
canonicity, normalization, and decidability. In both cases,
(U8)

allows us to leverage existing categorical machinery while still maintaining
the required strict equations.

Foundational assumptions.

Throughout, we work in a sufficiently strong metatheory to ensure that
Set

comes equipped with a collection of universes
e.g
.
, ZFC with the Grothendieck
universe axiom; we make use of the axiom of choice. We return to this topic
briefly in
Section

7.1
.

Acknowledgments

We are grateful to Steve Awodey, Thomas Streicher, and the anonymous referees
for helpful feedback and corrections to an earlier draft of this paper. This
research was supported by the United States Air Force Office of Scientific
Research under award numbers FA9550-21-1-0009 and FA9550-23-1-0728 (Tristan
Nguyen, program officer).

2
Reviewing Hofmann and Streicher’s universes

We begin by recalling constructions from
[
HS97
]
and
[
Str05
]

lifting universes from
Set
to Grothendieck topoi. To begin with, fix a
Grothendieck
universe

\mathsf{V}
, a transitive non-empty set closed under Kuratowski pairing,
power-sets, and
I
-indexed unions for each
I\in\mathsf{V}
.

2.1
Universes of sets

Each Grothendieck universe defines a universe as in
Definition

2
.

Construction 1
.

Define the universe
\mathcal{S}_{\mathsf{V}}\subseteq\operatorname{Hom}_{{\textbf{Set}}}
to be the collection of all morphisms

f\colon\hbox to34.01pt{\vbox to7.63pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 4.53471pt\lower-0.7919pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{}{}{}\lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-4.53471pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}

m

such that

m(a)=b
. By definition
q
fits into the triangle above, and an identical procedure extends it
to the required cartesian square
f
\boldsymbol{\upupsilon}
.

Remark 10
.

The above proof can be generalized to show that any universe in a boolean
topos satisfying
(U5)
satisfies
(U8)
.

Remark 11
.

In the category of sets, any universe in the sense of the present axioms
determines a universe in the sense of Grothendieck. Streicher’s axioms for
universes can therefore be thought of as a more
direct
alternative to
Grothendieck’s axioms, emphasizing ordinary mathematical constructions (
e.g
.
dependent product, sum, quotient) rather than set theoretical considerations
(transitive membership, power sets,
etc
.
).

. The construction of

being a

\mathrm{Pr}\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathscr{C}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle\mathscr{C}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathscr{C}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathscr{C}$}\mathclose{}{)}}_{{/\mathsf{y}\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle C$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle C$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle C$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle C$}\mathclose{}{)}}}}\simeq\mathrm{Pr}\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathscr{C}_{{/C}}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle\mathscr{C}_{{/C}}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathscr{C}_{{/C}}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathscr{C}_{{/C}}$}\mathclose{}{)}}
which represents the codomain fibration as a

to presheaves valued in the full subcategory of

\mathsf{V}
to induce an equivalence
\mathrm{Pr}_{\mathsf{V}}\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathscr{C}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle\mathscr{C}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathscr{C}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathscr{C}$}\mathclose{}{)}}_{{/\mathsf{y}\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle C$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle C$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle C$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle C$}\mathclose{}{)}}}}\simeq\mathrm{Pr}_{\mathsf{V}}\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathscr{C}_{{/C}}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle\mathscr{C}_{{/C}}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathscr{C}_{{/C}}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathscr{C}_{{/C}}$}\mathclose{}{)}}

The generic map

To check that
\check{\beta}
restricts along
\phi
to

Theorem 16
.

The class of morphisms
\hat{\mathcal{S}}_{\mathsf{V}}
in
\mathrm{Pr}\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathscr{C}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle\mathscr{C}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathscr{C}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathscr{C}$}\mathclose{}{)}}
is a universe satisfying

(U1–8)
.

2.3
Streicher’s universe of sheaves

Fixing a Grothendieck topology
J
on
\mathscr{C}
, we show that the universe
\hat{\mathcal{S}}_{\mathsf{V}}
induces a
universe on
\mathrm{Sh}\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathscr{C},J$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle\mathscr{C},J$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathscr{C},J$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathscr{C},J$}\mathclose{}{)}}
.
Let
i:\mathrm{Sh}\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathscr{C},J$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle\mathscr{C},J$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathscr{C},J$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathscr{C},J$}\mathclose{}{)}}\to\mathrm{Pr}\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathscr{C}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle\mathscr{C}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathscr{C}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathscr{C}$}\mathclose{}{)}}
denote the inclusion geometric morphism, so that
i_{*}
is the inclusion functor and
i^{*}
is sheafification.

Definition 17
.

We define
\tilde{\mathcal{S}}_{\mathsf{V}}
to be the collection of all maps
f
such that

i_{*}f\in\hat{\mathcal{S}}_{\mathsf{V}}
.

This collection of maps is easily shown to satisfy
(U1–4)
because

i_{*}
preserves finite limits. The existence of a generic map
(U5)
has
been the source of controversy within the type-theoretic literature; one
potential candidate is the restriction of
\pi_{\hat{\mathcal{S}}_{\mathsf{V}}}
to the presheaf of pointwise
\mathsf{V}
-small
sheaves, but this is not actually a sheaf as pointed out by

[
XE16
]
.

[
Str05
]
proposed a more direct approach: the generic map for

\tilde{\mathcal{S}}_{\mathsf{V}}
is the sheafification of the generic map for

\hat{\mathcal{S}}_{\mathsf{V}}
. To prove this, we recall Proposition 5.4.4 of
[
vdB11
]
:

Proposition 18
.

If
f\in\hat{\mathcal{S}}_{\mathsf{V}}
then
i^{*}f\in\tilde{\mathcal{S}}_{\mathsf{V}}
.

With this to hand, we immediately conclude that
i^{*}\boldsymbol{\upvarpi}\in\tilde{\mathcal{S}}_{\mathsf{V}}
.

Corollary 19
.

The family
i^{*}\boldsymbol{\upvarpi}
is generic for
\tilde{\mathcal{S}}_{\mathsf{V}}
.

Lemma 21
.

There exists a
\mathsf{V}
-small site
\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathscr{C},J$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle\mathscr{C},J$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathscr{C},J$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathscr{C},J$}\mathclose{}{)}}
such that
i^{*}\boldsymbol{\upvarpi}
does not
satisfy
(U8)
.

Proof.

Define
\mathscr{C}=\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{\{}\hbox{$\displaystyle 0\leq 1$}\mathclose{}{\}}}{\mathopen{}{\{}\hbox{$\textstyle 0\leq 1$}\mathclose{}{\}}}{\mathopen{}{\{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle 0\leq 1$}\mathclose{}{\}}}{\mathopen{}{\{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle 0\leq 1$}\mathclose{}{\}}}\times\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{\{}\hbox{$\displaystyle 0\leq 1$}\mathclose{}{\}}}{\mathopen{}{\{}\hbox{$\textstyle 0\leq 1$}\mathclose{}{\}}}{\mathopen{}{\{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle 0\leq 1$}\mathclose{}{\}}}{\mathopen{}{\{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle 0\leq 1$}\mathclose{}{\}}}
and let
J
be such
that
\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle 0,1$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle 0,1$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle 0,1$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle 0,1$}\mathclose{}{)}}
,
\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle 1,0$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle 1,0$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle 1,0$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle 1,0$}\mathclose{}{)}}
, and
\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle 1,1$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle 1,1$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle 1,1$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle 1,1$}\mathclose{}{)}}
have no non-trivial covers
while
\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle 0,0$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle 0,0$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle 0,0$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle 0,0$}\mathclose{}{)}}
is covered by the empty sieve. The sheafification functor

i^{*}\colon\hbox to189.8pt{\vbox to10pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 33.38899pt\lower-2.5pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{}{}{}\lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{

3
Generalities on descent and
\kappa
-compactness

In preparation for our universe construction, we recall notions of descent and compactness together
and develop the required theory. Accordingly, fix a Grothendieck topos

\mathscr{E}
. Unless specifically mentioned otherwise, we shall assume that all
regular cardinals are infinite.

In
Section

1
we observed that the natural notion of morphism between generic maps
\pi
,

\rho
for a universe is not a merely a commuting square
\pi
\rho
but rather a

cartesian
square; only the latter ensures that a family classified by
\pi
is also
classified by
\rho
. While
\mathscr{E}^{\to}
readily adopts the essential characteristics of
\mathscr{E}

(for instance, it is also a Grothendieck topos) the wide subcategory restricting to cartesian
squares is not even cocomplete. We first recall the descent properties of
\mathscr{E}
to show that this
subcategory is closed under coproducts, filtered colimits and pushouts along monomorphisms
(
Lemma

25
).

In
Section

2
we worked with a universe of presheaves valued in small sets. While
convenient, this definition of smallness relies on a choice of presentation of a topos as a
particular category of presheaves. Under mild restrictions, however,
\hat{\mathcal{S}}_{\mathsf{V}}
coincides
with the class of relatively
compact
morphisms. Compactness is a ‘presentation-invariant’
notion and thereby readily available in
\mathscr{E}
. We recall the theory of
\kappa
-compactness in

\mathscr{E}
. We show that for sufficiently large
\kappa
, the class of relatively
\kappa
-compact
morphisms form a universe satisfying
(U1–7)
closed under certain colimits
(
Lemmas

38
and
48
).

3.1
Descent in a Grothendieck topos

.

Proof.

We may first compute the colimit of
J
in the ordinary arrow category

\mathscr{E}^{\to}
. Next we must show that each map

J\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle d$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle d$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle d$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle d$}\mathclose{}{)}}
\operatorname{colim}_{\mathscr{D}}{J}
is cartesian, but this is exactly the
content of
J_{1}
enjoying descent. We must now check that the factorizations induced by the universal property of this colimit in
\mathscr{E}^{\to}

are cartesian.

Fixing a cartesian natural transformation
h\colon\hbox to94.61pt{\vbox to10pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\;\lower-2.5pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{}{}{}\lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{

{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-3.25346pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}

While all diagrams satisfy descent in an
\infty
-topos, only some diagrams in

1
-topos theory have descent. The following classes of colimits do enjoy
descent:

1.

Coproducts enjoy descent: this is one phrasing of the traditional
disjointness condition that for each
i\not=j
, the fiber product

X_{i}\times_{{\mathchoice{\textstyle\coprod}{\textstyle\coprod}{\scriptstyle\coprod}{\scriptscriptstyle\coprod}}_{k}X_{k}}X_{j}
is the initial object:

\mathbf{0}_{\mathscr{E}}
X_{j}
X_{i}
{\mathchoice{\textstyle\coprod}{\textstyle\coprod}{\scriptstyle\coprod}{\scriptscriptstyle\coprod}}_{k}{X_{k}}

2.

While pushouts do not generally enjoy descent (see

[
Rez10
, Example 2.3]
for a counterexample), pushouts along
monomorphisms do enjoy descent; this property is commonly referred to as

adhesivity

[
GL12a
]
.

3.

Filtered colimits enjoy descent.

The final condition (verified in
Lemma

27
) is a
generalization of the
exhaustivity
condition identified by

[
Shu15
]
.

Recall that filtered colimits commute with finite limits, so it suffices to check that the
following square below is cartesian for
d\to e
:

G\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle d$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle d$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle d$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle d$}\mathclose{}{)}}

(9)

But
Eq.

9
is cartesian because we have assumed that
G
F
is cartesian.

We recall the notion of
ideal diagram
from
[
AF05
]
.

Definition 28
.

An
ideal diagram
in a category
\mathscr{E}
is a functor

\mathscr{D}
\mathscr{E}
where
\mathscr{D}
is a small filtered preorder and the image
of each
d\leq e
is a monomorphism in
\mathscr{E}
.

Proof.

This follows for essentially the same reason as

Lemma

27
. Fixing

, to see that

is a monomorphism it suffices to check
that the following diagram is cartesian:

F\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle d$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle d$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle d$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle d$}\mathclose{}{)}}

Because filtered colimits commute with finite limits, it suffices to check

F\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle d$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle d$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle d$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle d$}\mathclose{}{)}}

But we have already assumed

to be a monomorphism.

Remark 30
.

For any regular cardinal

3.2
Compact objects and relatively compact maps

We recall some of the theory of compact objects. We refer the reader to
[
AR94
]

for a detailed exposition of compact objects and locally presentable categories.

Definition 32
.

An object
X\in\mathscr{E}
is said to be
\kappa
-compact
when the
functor
\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathscr{E}}\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle X,-$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle X,-$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle X,-$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle X,-$}\mathclose{}{)}}
preserves
\kappa
-filtered colimits. Following

[
Lur09
]
, a morphism
X
Y
is said to be
relatively

\kappa
-compact
if for each
\kappa
-compact object
Z
and
morphism
Z
Y
, the pullback
Z\times_{Y}X
is

\kappa
-compact:

Z\times_{Y}X
Z
X
Y

More tersely, the fibers of
X
Y
over
\kappa
-compact objects are

\kappa
-compact.

Remark 33
.

We note that the requirement that
X
\mathbf{1}
be relatively

\kappa
-compact is a priori stronger than merely asking
X
to be

\kappa
-compact. Their equivalence amounts to requiring
\kappa
-compact
objects to be closed under products, which will hold in all cases of
importance for us.

Notation 34
.

We will write
\mathcal{S}_{\kappa}
for the class of relatively
\kappa
-compact maps in
\mathscr{E}
.

Definition 35
.

A category
\mathscr{C}
is locally
\kappa
-presentable when
\mathscr{C}
is cocomplete and there is a set
of
\kappa
-compact objects that generates
\mathscr{C}
under
\kappa
-filtered colimits.

Notation 36
.

As a Grothendieck topos,
\mathscr{E}
is locally
\kappa
-presentable for some
regular cardinal
\kappa
. For the remainder of this subsection, we fix

\kappa
to be such a cardinal.

The colimit of a diagram in
\mathscr{E}^{\to}
of relatively
\kappa
-compact
morphisms is not necessarily relatively
\kappa
-compact. For
a simple counterexample, consider an object
X
that is
not

\kappa
-compact; then the following pushout of relatively
\kappa
-compact
morphisms is not relatively
\kappa
-compact:

\mathbf{1}
X
\mathbf{1}
\mathbf{1}
X
X
X
\mathbf{1}

More can be said when the diagram is cartesian (
i.e
.
valued in

\mathscr{E}^{\to}_{\mathit{cart}}
). In particular, relatively
\kappa
-compact
morphisms are closed under colimits of cartesian diagrams whose bases satisfy descent in
the sense of
Definition

22
, which we verify in

Lemma

38
. We first recall Proposition 4.18 of
[
Shu19
]
.

.

X
Y
i_{d}

Proof.

The only if direction is clear, so suppose for each
d\in\mathscr{D}
,

X\times_{Y}J\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle d$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle d$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle d$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle d$}\mathclose{}{)}}
J\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle d$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle d$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle d$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle d$}\mathclose{}{)}}
is relatively
\kappa
-compact. We must show that

Y
is relatively
\kappa
-compact.
Recall that any diagram can be presented as a
\kappa
-filtered diagram of

\kappa

. Therefore, it suffices to show that this holds when

First suppose

, we must show that the pullback

must factor through some

J\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle d$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle d$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle d$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle d$}\mathclose{}{)}}
Y
:

Z\times_{Y}X
Z
J\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle d$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle d$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle d$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle d$}\mathclose{}{)}}\times_{Y}X
J\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle d$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle d$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle d$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle d$}\mathclose{}{)}}
X
Y

By assumption,

\kappa

Next, suppose that

\kappa
-small diagram. In this case, the diagram category

\mathscr{E}^{\mathscr{D}}
is also locally
\kappa

[

, Corollary

. Accordingly,
D=\operatorname{colim}_{i\in\mathscr{I}}E_{i}
, where each

and by commutation of colimits

3.3
Relating small and relatively compact maps

i^{*}\dashv i_{*}
for the geometric embedding

\mathrm{Sh}\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathscr{C},J$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle\mathscr{C},J$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathscr{C},J$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathscr{C},J$}\mathclose{}{)}}
\mathrm{Pr}\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathscr{C}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle\mathscr{C}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathscr{C}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathscr{C}$}\mathclose{}{)}}
. Recall that a presheaf
P\in\mathrm{Pr}\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathscr{C}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle\mathscr{C}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathscr{C}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathscr{C}$}\mathclose{}{)}}

is
\kappa
-small when each
P(C)
is a
\kappa
-small set. Under mild
assumptions, small presheaves precisely correspond to compact presheaves. We
reproduce a proof due to
[
AR94
, Example 1.31]
:

Lemma 40
.

Given a regular cardinal
\kappa>\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{|}\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathscr{C}$}\mathclose{}{|}}{\mathopen{}{|}\hbox{$\textstyle\mathscr{C}$}\mathclose{}{|}}{\mathopen{}{|}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathscr{C}$}\mathclose{}{|}}{\mathopen{}{|}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathscr{C}$}\mathclose{}{|}}
and a presheaf
P\in\mathrm{Pr}\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathscr{C}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle\mathscr{C}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathscr{C}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathscr{C}$}\mathclose{}{)}}
, the latter is
\kappa
-compact if and
only if it is valued in
\kappa
-small sets.

Proof.

First express
P
as the colimit of representables:

P=\operatorname{colim}_{\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle c,p$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle c,p$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle c,p$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle c,p$}\mathclose{}{)}}\in\mathsf{Elt}\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle P$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle P$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle P$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle P$}\mathclose{}{)}}}\mathsf{y}\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle c$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle c$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle c$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle c$}\mathclose{}{)}}=\operatorname{colim}_{\mathsf{Elt}\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle P$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle P$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle P$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle P$}\mathclose{}{)}}}\mathsf{y}\circ\pi
.
On one hand, if
P
is valued in
\kappa
-small sets, then

\mathsf{Elt}\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle P$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle P$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle P$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle P$}\mathclose{}{)}}
is
\kappa
-small, while each
\mathsf{y}\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle c$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle c$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle c$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle c$}\mathclose{}{)}}
is

\kappa
-compact. Thus,
P
is a
\kappa
-small colimit of

\kappa
-compact objects, hence
\kappa
-compact.

On the other hand, suppose instead that
P
is
\kappa
-compact;
we will show that it is valued in
\kappa
-small sets.
By completing
\mathsf{Elt}\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle P$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle P$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle P$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle P$}\mathclose{}{)}}
under
\kappa
-small colimits and extending

\mathsf{y}\circ\pi
by colimits, we obtain a
\kappa
-filtered diagram
\mathscr{D}
and
a map
F\colon\hbox to91.33pt{\vbox to10pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 3.81944pt\lower-2.5pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{}{}{}\lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{

\mathsf{Elt}\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle R$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle R$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle R$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle R$}\mathclose{}{)}}

-compact.

For the next sequence of results, we shall require some results from the theory
of accessible categories and accessible functors. In order to state them, we
require a small amount of set-theoretic bureaucracy in the form of the

\mathrel{\rhd}
relation:

Definition 42
.

A cardinal
\lambda>\kappa
is
sharply larger
than
\kappa
, notated

\lambda\mathrel{\rhd}\kappa
, if each
\kappa
-accessible category is

\lambda
-accessible.

We emphasize that
\lambda\mathrel{\rhd}\kappa
is not the same as

\lambda>\kappa
nor does it mean anything akin to “
\lambda
is much larger
than
\kappa
”. We refer the reader to
[
AR94
, Theorem
2.11]
for more information about
\mathrel{\rhd}
. For our
purposes it suffices to know that if
\lambda
is strongly inaccessible then

\kappa<\lambda
is equivalent to
\kappa\mathrel{\lhd}\lambda
.

Lemma 43
.

There exists a cardinal
\lambda_{0}
such that for any

\lambda\mathrel{\rhd}\lambda_{0}
, both
i_{*}
and
i^{*}
preserve

\lambda
-filtered colimits and
\lambda
-compact objects.

Proof.

As adjoints
i_{*}
and
i^{*}
are both accessible functors. Therefore, the result follows immediately
from the uniformization result (2.19) of
[
AR94
]
.

Lemma 44
.

If
i^{*}
preserves
\lambda
-compact objects, then
i_{*}
reflects them.

Proof.

Let
E\in\mathrm{Sh}\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathscr{C},J$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle\mathscr{C},J$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathscr{C},J$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathscr{C},J$}\mathclose{}{)}}
be such that
i_{*}E
is
\lambda
-compact; because
i^{*}

preserves
\lambda
-compact objects,
i^{*}i_{*}E\cong E
is
\lambda
-compact.

Combining the above result with the characterization of
\kappa
-compact objects
given by
Lemma

40
, we deduce the following.

Corollary 45
.

Given a regular cardinal
\lambda
sharply larger than both

\lambda_{0}
and
\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{|}\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathscr{C}$}\mathclose{}{|}}{\mathopen{}{|}\hbox{$\textstyle\mathscr{C}$}\mathclose{}{|}}{\mathopen{}{|}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathscr{C}$}\mathclose{}{|}}{\mathopen{}{|}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathscr{C}$}\mathclose{}{|}}
, the following properties hold:

1.

\mathscr{E}
is locally
\lambda
-presentable.

2.

The
\lambda
-compact objects in
\mathscr{E}
are closed under finite limits.

If
\lambda
is further assumed to be strongly inaccessible, then we
additionally have:

3.

The set
\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathscr{E}}\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle X,Y$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle X,Y$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle X,Y$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle X,Y$}\mathclose{}{)}}
between two
\lambda
-compact objects
X,Y
is
\lambda
-small.

Lemma 46
.

Given a regular cardinal
\lambda
sharply larger than both
\lambda_{0}
and
\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{|}\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathscr{C}$}\mathclose{}{|}}{\mathopen{}{|}\hbox{$\textstyle\mathscr{C}$}\mathclose{}{|}}{\mathopen{}{|}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathscr{C}$}\mathclose{}{|}}{\mathopen{}{|}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathscr{C}$}\mathclose{}{|}}
,
the direct image functor
i_{*}
preserves and reflects
relatively
\lambda
-compact morphisms.

Proof.

We handle preservation and reflection separately.

Preservation.

Let
X

be a relatively
\lambda
-compact morphism in

. We

along with a map

Z
i_{*}Y
, it suffices to argue that the fiber product

is
\lambda

W
Z
i_{*}X
i_{*}Y

Observe that

Combining the above results with

, we obtain the following result:

Theorem 48
.

There exists a cardinal
\kappa
such that for any strongly inaccessible
\lambda\mathrel{\rhd}\kappa
,
\mathscr{E}
is locally

\lambda
-presentable and the class of relatively
\lambda
-compact maps in
\mathscr{E}
form a
universe
\mathcal{S}_{\lambda}
satisfying
(U1–7)
and
\lambda
-compact objects are closed under
finite limits.

Proof.

We define
\kappa
to be any regular cardinal sharply larger than both
\lambda_{0}
and
\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{|}\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathscr{C}$}\mathclose{}{|}}{\mathopen{}{|}\hbox{$\textstyle\mathscr{C}$}\mathclose{}{|}}{\mathopen{}{|}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathscr{C}$}\mathclose{}{|}}{\mathopen{}{|}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathscr{C}$}\mathclose{}{|}}
. We first recall that
\mathscr{E}

is locally
\lambda
-presentable and that
\lambda
-compact objects are closed under finite limits
by
Corollary

45
. Next,
Theorem

20
combined with

Lemmas

46
,
41
and
40

ensures that for any
\lambda\mathrel{\rhd}\kappa
, the universe
\mathcal{S}_{\lambda}
satisfies

(U1–6)
. Finally, we have established that
\mathcal{S}_{\lambda}
satisfies
(U7)
in

Lemma

39
.

Definition 49
.

We write
\mathfrak{c}\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathscr{E}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle\mathscr{E}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathscr{E}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathscr{E}$}\mathclose{}{)}}
for the cardinal
\kappa
provided by

Theorem

48
.

Corollary 50
.

For any strongly inaccessible
\lambda\mathrel{\rhd}\mathfrak{c}\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathscr{E}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle\mathscr{E}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathscr{E}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathscr{E}$}\mathclose{}{)}}
, the
full subcategory of
\mathscr{E}_{{/Y}}
spanned by relatively
\lambda
-compact
maps is essentially small.

Proof.

Lemma 51
.

For any strongly inaccessible
\lambda\mathrel{\rhd}\mathfrak{c}\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathscr{E}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle\mathscr{E}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathscr{E}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathscr{E}$}\mathclose{}{)}}
,
there exists a
\lambda
-small set of monomorphisms
\mathcal{I}
generating all
monomorphisms in
\mathscr{E}
under pushout, transfinite composition, and retracts.
Moreover, the domains and codomains of morphisms in
\mathcal{I}
are
\lambda
-compact.

Proof.

[
Bek00
, Proposition 1.12]
shows that the collection of sub-quotients of representables

J
generate all monomorphisms in
\mathrm{Pr}\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathscr{C}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle\mathscr{C}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathscr{C}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathscr{C}$}\mathclose{}{)}}
. Explicitly,
J
is the collection of
monomorphisms
A
B
where
B
is the quotient of a representable
\mathsf{y}\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle C$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle C$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle C$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle C$}\mathclose{}{)}}
. As

\mathrm{Pr}\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathscr{C}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle\mathscr{C}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathscr{C}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathscr{C}$}\mathclose{}{)}}
is both well-powered and co-well-powered there is essentially a set of such
monomorphisms.

A quotient of a representable
\mathsf{y}\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle C$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle C$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle C$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle C$}\mathclose{}{)}}
is determined by a morphism

\mathsf{y}\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle C$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle C$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle C$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle C$}\mathclose{}{)}}\times\mathsf{y}\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle C$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle C$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle C$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle C$}\mathclose{}{)}}
\Omega
. As
\lambda>\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{|}\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathscr{C}$}\mathclose{}{|}}{\mathopen{}{|}\hbox{$\textstyle\mathscr{C}$}\mathclose{}{|}}{\mathopen{}{|}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathscr{C}$}\mathclose{}{|}}{\mathopen{}{|}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathscr{C}$}\mathclose{}{|}}
,
\Omega
is
\lambda
-small
and there is a
\lambda
-small set of representables therefore
J
may be chosen to be

\lambda
-small. Finally, the domains and codomains of monomorphisms in
J
are
\lambda
-small,
since they are subquotients of representables which are
\lambda
-small;
and by
Lemma

40
, this implies they are
\lambda
-compact.

We now define
\mathcal{I}\subset\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathscr{E}}
as the image of
J
under
i^{*}
. As
i_{*}
preserves
monomorphisms and
i^{*}
preserves all colimits,
\mathcal{I}
generates all monomorphisms in
\mathscr{E}

under pushout, transfinite composition, and retracts. The domains and codomains of morphisms in

\mathcal{I}
are seen to be
\lambda
-compact by
Lemma

43
.

4
Main result: a universe satisfying realignment

Let
\mathscr{E}
be a Grothendieck topos and fix a strongly inaccessible cardinal

\kappa\mathrel{\rhd}\mathfrak{c}\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathscr{E}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle\mathscr{E}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathscr{E}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathscr{E}$}\mathclose{}{)}}
.
We have previously shown that
\mathcal{S}_{\kappa}
satisfies
(U1–7)
. We construct a new generic
map for this class and thereby conclude that
\mathcal{S}_{\kappa}
satisfies
(U8)
.

4.1

4.2
A small object argument

In this section we construct a candidate for the generic family
of
\mathcal{S}_{\kappa}
, using a variant of the small object argument. Our
construction is very similar to that of

[
Shu15
,
Shu19
]
but relies on different assumptions.

By
Lemma

51
, there is a
\kappa
-small set of monomorphisms

\mathcal{I}\subseteq\mathscr{E}^{\rightarrowtail}
generating all the monomorphisms of
\mathscr{E}
under
pushout, transfinite composition, and retracts, and whose domains and codomains are
\kappa
-compact.

We proceed by induction on ordinals
\alpha\leq\kappa

\kappa
-compact by

38
.
Next we check that

\kappa
-compact

\pi_{\kappa}^{\alpha}
is relatively
\kappa
-compact. We may

38
because

28
enjoys descent
as a pushout along a monomorphism, so it suffices to check that each node of

Eq.

28
is relatively
\kappa
-compact. We have already
assumed that
\pi_{\kappa}^{\alpha}
is relatively
\kappa
-compact; both

{\mathchoice{\textstyle\coprod}{\textstyle\coprod}{\scriptstyle\coprod}{\scriptscriptstyle\coprod}}_{d\in\mathsf{D}_{\kappa}\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle\pi_{\kappa}^{\alpha}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle\pi_{\kappa}^{\alpha}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\pi_{\kappa}^{\alpha}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\pi_{\kappa}^{\alpha}$}\mathclose{}{)}}}{f_{d}}

because coproducts

are relatively

In the limit case we assume

\kappa

\kappa

again, since

\mathcal{O}_{<\alpha}
is a filtered preorder and therefore its diagrams
enjoy descent (
Lemma

27
).

.

The transfinite composition
\pi_{\kappa}\coloneqq\operatorname{colim}_{\mathcal{O}_{<\kappa}}{\pi_{\kappa}^{\bullet}}

\kappa

By

Corollary 63
.

All monomorphisms are realignable,
i.e
.
we have
\mathcal{J}_{\pi_{\kappa}}=\mathscr{E}^{\rightarrowtail}
.

Proof.

We have assumed that
\mathcal{I}
generates
\mathscr{E}^{\rightarrowtail}
under
pushout, transfinite composition, and retracts; but
\mathcal{J}_{\pi_{\kappa}}
is
saturated (

), so our result follows from the fact that
generating monomorphisms are realignable (
Lemma

62
).

Corollary 64
.

\mathcal{S}_{\kappa}
is a universe satisfying
(U1–8)
.

4.4
A cumulative universe hierarchy

Fix a second strongly inaccessible cardinal
\mu>\kappa

\pi_{\mu}
for

satisfying
(U8)
by the same small object argument detailed in

Genericity of
\pi_{\mu}
implies that we automatically obtain a cartesian morphism

\pi_{\kappa}
\pi_{\mu}
but this map is not generally a monomorphism. On the other hand, we can
choose our own cartesian monomorphism
\pi_{\kappa}
\pi_{\mu}
by means of a pointwise
construction.

Lemma 66
.

U_{\kappa}
is
\mu
-compact.

Proof.

\mu

\mu

\mu

U_{\kappa}^{\alpha}
is
\mu
-compact for each
\alpha<\kappa

We show this by transfinite induction on
\alpha<\kappa

U_{\kappa}^{\alpha}
is then a
\mu
-small colimit of
\mu
-compact objects. Fix
\alpha<\kappa

and assume that
U_{\kappa}^{\alpha}
is
\mu
-small.

5
Relating internal formulations of realignment

We have focused on the external formulation of realignment as a property of a
class of maps; recent years have seen several applications of type-theoretic
formulation of realignment that employs the internal language of a topos. In

Section

5.1
we discuss a logical formulation popularized by
Orton and Pitts, which we compare with a more geometrical formulation due to
Sterling in
Section

5.2
that mirrors the recollement of a space from
open and closed subspaces, completing the latent analogy with Artin gluing.

5.1
Internal realignment à la Orton and Pitts

In another guise,
[
Coh+17
]
has employed the realignment property in the
cubical set model of cubical type theory, later rephrased into the internal
language of topoi by
[
Bir+16
]
and employed by

[
OP16
]
to give more abstract and general constructions of
models of cubical type theory in presheaf topoi.

In what follows, we fix a universe
\mathcal{S}
satisfying
(U1–5)
such that,
in particular, there is a generic map
\pi\colon\hbox to32.79pt{\vbox to7.63pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 3.97916pt\lower-0.7919pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{}{}{}\lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces

Notation 68
.

For any

, an

of
B
is defined to be a type

f:A\cong B
. We will write
\mathsf{Iso}_{\mathcal{S}}\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle B$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle B$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle B$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle B$}\mathclose{}{)}}\coloneqq{\mathchoice{\textstyle\sum}{\textstyle\sum}{\scriptstyle\sum}{\scriptscriptstyle\sum}}_{A:U}{A\cong B}
for the type of isomorphs of

,
and
\mathsf{Iso}_{\mathcal{S}}\coloneqq{\mathchoice{\textstyle\sum}{\textstyle\sum}{\scriptstyle\sum}{\scriptscriptstyle\sum}}_{B:U}\mathsf{Iso}_{\mathcal{S}}\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle B$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle B$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle B$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle B$}\mathclose{}{)}}
for the object of
isomorphisms.

Notation 69
.

We will write
X^{+}
for the partial map classifier
{\mathchoice{\textstyle\sum}{\textstyle\sum}{\scriptstyle\sum}{\scriptscriptstyle\sum}}_{\phi:\Omega}X^{\phi}
, and
\eta^{+}:X\to X^{+}
for its unit.

Definition 70
.

A
realignment structure
is defined to
be an element of the dependent type

{\mathchoice{\textstyle\prod}{\textstyle\prod}{\scriptstyle\prod}{\scriptscriptstyle\prod}}_{B:U}{\mathchoice{\textstyle\prod}{\textstyle\prod}{\scriptstyle\prod}{\scriptscriptstyle\prod}}_{A:\mathsf{Iso}_{\mathcal{S}}\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle G$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle G$}\mathclose{}{)}}$}\mathclose{}{\}}}{\mathopen{}{\{}\hbox{$\textstyle .
The
realignment axiom
on
U
postulates the existence of a realignment
structure.

Combining the application described in

Section

6.3
with the internal perspective of

[
OP16
]
, the realignment operation is included as
an axiom of
synthetic Tait computability

[
Ste21
]
,
the mathematical framework behind the recent normalization result for cubical
type theory
[
SA21
]
.

We demonstrate in
Lemmas

72
and
73
that
the existence of realignment structures in the sense of

Definition

70
is equivalent to the realignment property of

Definition

4
.

Notation 71
.

We will write

\mathsf{Iso}_{\mathcal{S}}^{*}:\mathscr{E}_{{/\mathsf{Iso}_{\mathcal{S}}}}
for the dependent type

I:\mathsf{Iso}_{\mathcal{S}}\vdash\pi_{1}\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle I$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle I$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle I$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle I$}\mathclose{}{)}}
of pointed isomorphisms.
We define the type
\mathsf{Desc}_{\mathcal{S}}
of
\mathcal{S}
-
realignment data
to be the
dependent sum
{\mathchoice{\textstyle\sum}{\textstyle\sum}{\scriptstyle\sum}{\scriptscriptstyle\sum}}_{B:U}\mathsf{Iso}_{\mathcal{S}}\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle B$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle B$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle B$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle B$}\mathclose{}{)}}^{+}
. We will write

\mathsf{Desc}_{\mathcal{S}}^{*}:\mathscr{E}_{{/\mathsf{Desc}_{\mathcal{S}}}}

for the dependent type
D:\mathsf{Desc}_{\mathcal{S}}\vdash\pi_{1}\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle D$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle D$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle D$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle D$}\mathclose{}{)}}
of pointed realignment data.

Lemma 72
.

Let
\mathcal{S}
be a universe satisfying
(U8)
for the class of all
monomorphisms; then
\mathcal{S}
has a realignment structure.

Proof.

We have a cartesian monomorphism
\mathsf{Iso}_{\mathcal{S}}^{*}
\mathsf{Desc}_{\mathcal{S}}^{*}

that turns an isomorphism into the corresponding
total
realignment datum
with
\phi\coloneqq\top
. Taking the domain of an isomorphism
corresponds to a cartesian map
\mathsf{Iso}_{\mathcal{S}}^{*}
\pi
.
Combining these, we may rephrase
Definition

70
as the existence of
a cartesian morphism
\mathsf{Desc}_{\mathcal{S}}^{*}
\pi
in the following
configuration:

\mathsf{Iso}_{\mathcal{S}}^{*}
\mathsf{Desc}_{\mathcal{S}}^{*}
\pi

(32)

The dotted map of
Eq.

32
exists by the realignment
axiom because
\mathsf{Desc}_{\mathcal{S}}^{*}\in\mathcal{S}
.

Lemma 73
.

Suppose that
\mathcal{S}
has a realignment structure; then
\mathcal{S}
satisfies

(U8)
for the class of all monomorphisms.

Proof.

We transform external realignment problems into internal ones. Fix a span of cartesian maps as below such that
f\in\mathcal{S}
:

h
f
\pi
\Phi
\Gamma
U
p_{\phi}
A

(33)

Because
f\in\mathcal{S}
, we additionally have:

h
\Phi
f
\Gamma
p_{\phi}
\pi
U
B

(34)

We take the characteristic map of
\Phi
:

\Phi
\Gamma
p_{\phi}
\mathbf{1}_{\mathscr{E}}
\Omega
\top
\phi

(35)

We have a map
\Phi
\mathsf{Iso}_{\mathcal{S}}\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle B\circ p_{\phi}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle B\circ p_{\phi}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle B\circ p_{\phi}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle B\circ p_{\phi}$}\mathclose{}{)}}
determined by
A
,
which we observe forms the base of a cartesian map
h
\mathsf{Iso}_{\mathcal{S}}^{*}
. On
the other hand, we have a map
\Gamma
\mathsf{Iso}_{\mathcal{S}}\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle B$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle B$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle B$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle B$}\mathclose{}{)}}^{+}
,
i.e
.
a partial
isomorphism with support
\phi
between
A
and
B\circ p_{\phi}
.
Therefore we
have a realignment datum
\Gamma
\mathsf{Desc}_{\mathcal{S}}
determined by
B
and our
partial isomorphism; in fact, this is the base of a cartesian map

f
\mathsf{Desc}_{\mathcal{S}}^{*}
which we may compose with the realignment
structure to obtain the desired factorization:

\mathsf{Iso}_{\mathcal{S}}^{*}
\mathsf{Desc}_{\mathcal{S}}^{*}
h
f
\pi

(36)

In short, we solved the realignment problem by restricting from the
generic
case.

Question 1
.

Can
E

Eq.

37
to make the

Although identity of objects is not properly part of the language of category
theory, it becomes meaningful when considering
internal categories
as we
do in
Section

5.2.3
below. We will see that the realignment
axiom
(U8)

strictly.

5.2.3
Internal recollement

\mathcal{U}

It is not difficult to verify that the existence of such a type constructor is
equivalent to the internal realignment axiom discussed in
Section

5.1
.

Lemma 74
.

Let
G
be a realignment structure for
U
in the sense of

Definition

70
; then there exists a
\mathsf{Glue}
connective
satisfying the described rules.

Proof.

Let
O,K
as above and consider the application of
G
to
B\coloneqq{\mathchoice{\textstyle\sum}{\textstyle\sum}{\scriptstyle\sum}{\scriptscriptstyle\sum}}_{x:J_{*}O}Kx
and the partial isomorphism
z:J\vdash B\cong Oz
,
which exists because each fiber of
K
is
J
-connected. From this pair we
thus obtain both
\mathsf{Glue}\,J\,O\,K
and
\mathsf{glue}\,J\,O\,K
.

Az$}\mathclose{}{)}}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle\pi_{2}\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle Az$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle Az$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle Az$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle Az$}\mathclose{}{)}}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\pi_{2}\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle Az$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle Az$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle Az$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle Az$}\mathclose{}{)}}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\pi_{2}\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle Az$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle Az$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle Az$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle Az$}\mathclose{}{)}}$}\mathclose{}{)}}\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle xz$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle xz$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle xz$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle xz$}\mathclose{}{)}}=y$}\mathclose{}{\}}}
. Then we consider the total
isomorph given by the pair
\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathsf{Glue}\,J\,O\,K,\pi_{2}\circ\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathsf{glue}\,J\,O\,K$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle\mathsf{glue}\,J\,O\,K$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathsf{glue}\,J\,O\,K$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{glue}\,J\,O\,K$}\mathclose{}{)}}^{-1}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle\mathsf{Glue}\,J\,O\,K,\pi_{2}\circ\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathsf{glue}\,J\,O\,K$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle\mathsf{glue}\,J\,O\,K$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathsf{glue}\,J\,O\,K$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{glue}\,J\,O\,K$}\mathclose{}{)}}^{-1}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathsf{Glue}\,J\,O\,K,\pi_{2}\circ\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathsf{glue}\,J\,O\,K$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle\mathsf{glue}\,J\,O\,K$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathsf{glue}\,J\,O\,K$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{glue}\,J\,O\,K$}\mathclose{}{)}}^{-1}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{Glue}\,J\,O\,K,\pi_{2}\circ\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathsf{glue}\,J\,O\,K$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle\mathsf{glue}\,J\,O\,K$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathsf{glue}\,J\,O\,K$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{glue}\,J\,O\,K$}\mathclose{}{)}}^{-1}$}\mathclose{}{)}}
.

The benefit of the present axiomatization is that a family of types being
fiberwise
J
-connected is a
property
; in contrast, the Orton–Pitts
axiomatization (
Definition

70
) requires every use of realignment to be accompanied by a
chosen isomorphism. We have gained significant experience with both
axiomatizations in the context of synthetic Tait
computability
[
Ste21
,
SH21
,
SA21
,
Gra22
,
SH22
,
Niu+22
]
,
and found that the present one is substantially simpler to use in practice.

6
Applications of realignment

An immediate consequence of
Section

4
is an interpretation of
Martin-Löf type theory with a cumulative hierarchy of universes in arbitrary
Grothendieck topoi (recall that we have assumed a hierarchy of Grothendieck
universes). In fact, the new interpretation of Martin-Löf type
theory in Grothendieck topoi enables more direct independence proofs for various
axioms such as Markov’s principle. But the realignment property itself has
played an important role in the semantics of homotopy type theory as developed
by
[
Awo21
]
, Kapulkin, Lumsdaine, and Voevodsky
[
KL21
]
,
[
Str14
,
Ste19
,
Shu15
,
Shu19
]
.
In particular, realignment appears to be a necessary ingredient for
constructing a fibrant and univalent universe. The same principle is employed
by
[
SAG22
, Lemma 5.33]
in their proof of

canonicity
for XTT, a variant of cubical type theory: in particular,

op. cit
.
used a special case of
(U8)
to realign codes in the universe of
an Artin gluing over chosen codes in the universe of its open subtopos.

6.1
Independence results for Martin-Löf type theory

Sheaf semantics has historically been employed to prove independence results
for various forms of logic; the use of sheaf semantics to verify the analogous
results for dependent type theory with universes has been hampered by the
(now-resolved) difficulties in constructing well-behaved universes in sheaf
topoi. These difficulties have motivated two somewhat less direct methods for
proving independence results: constructing
operational
or

relational
models of type theory using the Beth–Kripke–Joyal sheaf
semantics of predicate logic
[
CM16
]
, or by constructing
denotational models of type theory in
stacks
rather than
sheaves
[
CMR17
]
. The present work provides a more
direct approach, as the presence of universes validating
(U1–8)

ensures a simple and direct denotational semantics of dependent type theory in
sheaves. We illustrate this through a concrete example and sketch a simpler
proof of the independence of Markov’s principle.

6.1.1
Independence of Markov’s principle

Markov’s principle states that for any decidable property
P\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle x$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle x$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle x$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle x$}\mathclose{}{)}}
of natural
numbers, the proposition
\exists x.P{x}
is
\lnot\lnot
-stable:

\forall P:\mathbb{N}\to\mathbf{2}.{\lnot\lnot\exists x.Px=0}\to\exists x.Px=0

Formalized in the language of dependent type theory, Markov’s principle is
rendered by
[
CM16
]
equivalently as the existence of a
global element of the following type:

{\mathchoice{\textstyle\prod}{\textstyle\prod}{\scriptstyle\prod}{\scriptscriptstyle\prod}}_{P:\mathbb{N}\to\mathbf{2}}\mathchoice{\mathopen{\mathchoice{\big(}{\big(}{\big(}{(}}\hbox{$\displaystyle\lnot\lnot{{\mathchoice{\textstyle\sum}{\textstyle\sum}{\scriptstyle\sum}{\scriptscriptstyle\sum}}_{x:\mathbb{N}}Px=0}\to{\mathchoice{\textstyle\sum}{\textstyle\sum}{\scriptstyle\sum}{\scriptscriptstyle\sum}}_{x:\mathbb{N}}Px=0$}\mathclose{\mathchoice{\big)}{\big)}{\big)}{)}}}{\mathopen{\mathchoice{\big(}{\big(}{\big(}{(}}\hbox{$\textstyle\lnot\lnot{{\mathchoice{\textstyle\sum}{\textstyle\sum}{\scriptstyle\sum}{\scriptscriptstyle\sum}}_{x:\mathbb{N}}Px=0}\to{\mathchoice{\textstyle\sum}{\textstyle\sum}{\scriptstyle\sum}{\scriptscriptstyle\sum}}_{x:\mathbb{N}}Px=0$}\mathclose{\mathchoice{\big)}{\big)}{\big)}{)}}}{\mathopen{\mathchoice{\big(}{\big(}{\big(}{(}}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\lnot\lnot{{\mathchoice{\textstyle\sum}{\textstyle\sum}{\scriptstyle\sum}{\scriptscriptstyle\sum}}_{x:\mathbb{N}}Px=0}\to{\mathchoice{\textstyle\sum}{\textstyle\sum}{\scriptstyle\sum}{\scriptscriptstyle\sum}}_{x:\mathbb{N}}Px=0$}\mathclose{\mathchoice{\big)}{\big)}{\big)}{)}}}{\mathopen{\mathchoice{\big(}{\big(}{\big(}{(}}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\lnot\lnot{{\mathchoice{\textstyle\sum}{\textstyle\sum}{\scriptstyle\sum}{\scriptscriptstyle\sum}}_{x:\mathbb{N}}Px=0}\to{\mathchoice{\textstyle\sum}{\textstyle\sum}{\scriptstyle\sum}{\scriptscriptstyle\sum}}_{x:\mathbb{N}}Px=0$}\mathclose{\mathchoice{\big)}{\big)}{\big)}{)}}}

The independence of Markov’s principle from intuitionistic higher-order logic
is established easily by considering the internal logic of the topos of sheaves
on Cantor space
\mathcal{C}
,
i.e
.
the space of infinite binary sequences
equipped with the product topology. If
\mathrm{Sh}\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathcal{C}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle\mathcal{C}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathcal{C}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{C}$}\mathclose{}{)}}
did not model
universes, we would not however be able to use it directly to verify the
independence of Markov’s principle from Martin-Löf type theory with
universes.
Our result concerning universes in Grothendieck topoi, however, allows one to
immediately deduce the independence of Markov’s principle from Martin-Löf
type theory with universes without needing to pass to the significantly more
complex stack semantics of
[
CMR17
]
, bypassing as well
the detour through operational semantics of
[
CM16
]
.

Corollary 76
.

Neither Markov’s principle nor its negation is derivable in Martin-Löf type theory
with a cumulative hierarchy of strict universes.

6.2
Semantics of the univalent universes

The semantics of univalent universes has proved to be a crucial technical
difficulty in models of homotopy type theory and cubical type theory; in
paticular, it is necessary to translate facts between the language of model
category theory and the language of universes. We briefly illustrate how
judicious application of
(U8)
has been used in the literature to
entirely eliminate these
difficulties
[
Str14
,
KL21
,
Shu15
,
Shu19
,
Awo21
]
.
In fact, this observation was the original motivation for

[
Shu15
]
to isolate
(U8)
.

We illustrate the utility of
(U8)
by tracing through the salient
aspects of the model given by Kapulkin, Lumsdaine, and Voevodsky
[
KL21
]
and defer to

[
Shu15
,
Shu19
]
for a more systematic approach. Concretely, we
will work in
sSet
and fix a pair of strongly inaccessible cardinals

\kappa_{0}<\kappa_{1}
inducing universes
\mathcal{V}_{0}\subseteq\mathcal{V}_{1}

each satisfying
(U1–8)
. Moreover, by
Section

4.4
, we can
choose a generic map for
\mathcal{V}_{0}
whose base lies in
\mathcal{V}_{1}
.

Let
\mathcal{U}_{i}\subseteq\mathcal{V}_{i}
be the class of Kan fibrations in

\mathcal{V}_{i}
.

Lemma 77
.

The class of maps
\mathcal{U}_{i}
satisfies
(U1,3,4,8)
.

Proof.

(U1,3)
follow immediately from the fact that
\mathcal{V}_{i}

satisfies
(U1,3)
and that any right-orthogonal class is closed under
composition and pullback.
(U4)
is an immediate consequence of the
right-properness of the Kan-Quillen model structure.

To show that
\mathcal{U}_{i}
satisfies
(U8)
, we being by fixing a
generic family

is a Kan
fibration. This is a well-defined simplicial set because Kan fibrations are stable

.

\alpha

\mathsf{Ex}_{\infty}
functor. A near immediate consequence of

U_{\mathcal{U}_{0}}

Theorem 79
.

The object
U_{\mathcal{U}_{0}}
lies within
\mathcal{U}_{1}

Proof.

As a subobject of
\mathcal{V}_{0}

\mathcal{V}_{1}

\Lambda^{n}_{i}
\Delta^{n}
U_{\mathcal{U}_{0}}
\alpha

\mathcal{U}_{0}

\pi_{\mathcal{U}_{0}}
is univalent. Prior to discussing the proof of
univalence, we must fix a few definitions.

We will now sketch the proof that
\pi_{\mathcal{U}_{0}}
is univalent. Just
as with
Theorem

79
, the proof decomposes into two pieces: a
homotopy-theoretic result and a careful analysis and application of

(U8)
to parlay this result into the appropriate result on the
universe. For univalence, the relevant homotopy-theoretic fact is the

equivalence extension property
, apparently first isolated by Kapulkin, Lumsdaine, and Voevodsky
[
KL21
]
, named by Awodey,
and further developed by several authors including Awodey, Coquand, Sattler, and Shulman
[
Shu15
,
Shu19
,
Sat17
,
Awo21
,
Coh+17
]
.

6.2.1
Lemma (Equivalence Extension Property)

We consider a diagram of the following shape, in which the downward maps are Kan fibrations,
i\colon\hbox to32.5pt{\vbox to7.63pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 3.75pt\lower-0.7919pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{}{}{}\lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-3.75pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}

6.3
Artin gluing and synthetic Tait computability

Artin gluing is used by computer scientists to prove metatheorems for type
theories and programming languages such as normalization, canonicity,
decidability, parametricity, conservativity, and computational adequacy.

[
SH21
]
have introduced
synthetic Tait
computability
as an abstraction for working in the internal language of
glued topoi, taking the realignment law
(U8)
in its internal
form (see
Section

5
) as a basic axiom.

6.3.1
History and motivation

Synthetic Tait computability (or
STC
) was first employed in
op. cit
.
to
prove a generalized abstraction/parametricity theorem for a language of
software packages (“modules”) in the style of Standard ML; subsequently,

[
SA21
]
used STC to positively resolve the long-standing

normalization conjecture
for cubical type
theory
[
Ang+21
]
.
4
4

4

See also Sterling’s
dissertation
[
Ste21
]
for a more detailed treatment of both this result and synthetic Tait computability in general.

Building on these results,
[
Gra22
]
adapted STC to
verify the analogous conjecture for
multimodal type
theory

[
Gra+20
]
. In their original formulation, all of these results relied
heavily on
(U8)
, but the glued topoi in the cited results were all of
presheaf type and hence the presheaf-theoretic universes of

[
HS97
]
could be brought to bear without broaching the
question of strict universes in sheaf topoi.

More recently, synthetic Tait computability has been employed in scenarios
where the glued topos is not known to be of presheaf type. For example,

[
GB22
]
proved a canonicity result for a version of

guarded dependent type theory
for which the necessary instance of STC
involved a Grothendieck topology. It has therefore become a matter
of some urgency to verify the existence of universes satisfying
(U1-8)

in arbitrary Grothendieck topoi.

6.3.2

{}{}}

be the corresponding open immersion of
topoi. Fixing a universe
\mathcal{S}
in
\mathscr{G}
(
i.e
.
a class of maps satisfying

(U1–7)
) that contains
j_{*}\mathcal{T}
, we may define a new
pre-universe
\mathcal{U}
consisting of the subclass of
\mathcal{S}
spanned by maps

f
with
j^{*}f\in\mathcal{T}
.

We wish to verify that
\mathcal{U}
likewise carries the structure of a model
of Martin-Löf type theory in the same sense of satisfying
(U1,3–5)
;
results of this kind are used to prove important syntactic metatheorems for
type theories, such as canonicity (a type theoretic analogue to the existence
property), normalization, decidability of judgmental equality, and
conservativity.

Lemma 83
.

The class of maps
\mathcal{U}\subseteq\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathscr{G}}
satisfies
(U1,3,4)
.

Proof.

This is a straightforward consequence of the fact that
j^{*}
is a logical
functor, using the fact that
\mathcal{T}
and
\mathcal{S}

Theorem 84

This property is particularly useful in proofs of metatheorems of type theories

In this context, one typically requires not only that

, these strict
equations would preclude a conceptual construction of these codes.

Remark 85
.

\Citet

uemura:2017 presents an alternative construction for a pre-universe in

\mathscr{G}
satisfying
Theorem

84
. Rather than relying on

(U8)
,
[
Uem17
]
begins with separate pre-universes from
\mathscr{E}
and

\mathscr{F}
and combines them directly. This explicit decomposition ensures that
the resultant universe satisfies the special case of
(U8)
necessary
for
Theorem

84
.

7
Conclusions and future work

We have shown that every Grothendieck topos can be equipped with a cumulative
hierarchy of universes satisfying
(U1–8)
assuming sufficient
universes in the background set theory. This result is important because it
extends the Hofmann–Streicher interpretation of Martin-Löf type theory in
presheaf topoi to arbitrary sheaf topoi.

7.1
Prospects for a constructive version

Our constructions are highly classical; in particular, we rely on the theory of
locally presentable categories and
\kappa
-compactness, both of which make
heavy use of choice. Developing a constructively acceptable version of

Section

4
remains an open problem. We briefly survey the landscape of
universes within a particular constructive metatheory: the internal language of
an elementary topos
\mathscr{E}
.

Although the literal definition of a Grothendieck universe is meaningless in

\mathscr{E}
, we can proceed analogously and fix a generic map
\widetilde{\mathsf{V}}
\mathsf{V}

satisfying the appropriate version of
(U2–4,6)
. The class
\mathcal{S}_{\mathsf{V}}

classified by this map then satisfies
(U1–6)
. Already some care must
be taken; without choice, a family with
\mathsf{V}
-small fibers need not be
classified by a map into
\mathsf{V}
. Absent the law of the excluded middle,

(U8)
is satisfied for at least the class of

decidable
monomorphisms
A
B
.

The Hofmann–Streicher construction exposed in
Section

2
works over
\mathscr{E}
without
modification. In particular, the standard generic
family of
\mathcal{S}_{\mathsf{V}}
lifts to a universe in the category of internal presheaves

\mathrm{Pr}_{\mathscr{E}}\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathscr{C}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle\mathscr{C}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathscr{C}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathscr{C}$}\mathclose{}{)}}
for any
\mathsf{V}
-small internal category
\mathscr{C}
. The class of maps

\hat{\mathcal{S}}_{\mathsf{V}}
classified by this map satisfies

(U1–6)
.
(U8)
is satisfied only for the class of

level-wise
decidable monomorphisms: monomorphisms
A
B
whose components

{}{}}}{
{}{}}

}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-27.04361pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}

are all decidable
[
OP16
]
. In fact,
[
Swa18
]
shows that this
result is sharp: it is possible to choose a base topos in such a way that this generic map cannot satisfy
(U8)
for all
monomorphisms, though it remains possible that there is
another
generic map satisfying

(U8)
for all monomorphisms.
Finally, this universe induces a universe
\tilde{\mathcal{S}}_{\mathsf{V}}
in any sheaf subtopos

\mathrm{Sh}_{\mathscr{E}}\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathscr{C},J$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle\mathscr{C},J$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathscr{C},J$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathscr{C},J$}\mathclose{}{)}}
. The construction is identical to that of
Section

2
and
\tilde{\mathcal{S}}_{\mathsf{V}}
satisfies
(U1–6)
just as in the classical setting. In
this setting, however, the status of
(U8)
remains entirely open for
this universe.

Over a base topos
\mathscr{E}
not satisyfing the axiom of choice, it is reasonable to hope
that properties such as
(U7)
or
(U8)
might lift from
\mathscr{E}

to any topos bounded over
\mathscr{E}
; this lifting is verified for
(U7)

in the context of
algebraic set
theory

[
JM95
,
vdB11
]
, but the corresponding
lifting for
(U8)
remains a conjecture.
